In our simulation study, we provide some first suggestions why it is important to incorporate covariates that affect the entire network (common drivers). In our example, we see that ignoring such common drivers leads to less accurate results about the estimation of the influence structure.

Further research directions include the discussion of covariates in the excitation kernels
g
. In our simulations, estimation of
\gamma
appears to be a difficult task even though our model assumes this excitation to be the same for all interactions. Nevertheless, it is arguably quite interesting for applications to assume that the interactions might be heterogeneous. It is then of interest to learn these interactions. A more direct question, would be to generalize the results to multiple observations, where one observes several multivariate Hawkes processes with the same set of parameters but potentially different covariates.

Appendix.

Appendix
A
discusses details about the implementation of the procedure presented in this paper. Appendix
B
contains theoretical results about Hawkes processes that are useful for our theory. Afterwards, Appendix
C
presents proofs that were left out in the main paper. In Appendix
D
, we give a heuristic discussion for the necessity of the de-biasing step. And in Appendix
E
, we discuss the random compatibility conditions in a special case. In Appendix
F
, we present additional details for the proofs of Section
B.2
. Appendix
G
contains the proof of Theorem
3.2
and further details for the proof of Lemma
C.1
. Appendix
H
contains proofs needed in Section
3.2
. Appendix
I
provides more technical details for the proofs of results from Section
3.3
; and, finally, Appendix
J
presents additional simulation results.

Appendix A
Algorithmic and computational considerations

A.1
Using the LAR algorithm

In this Section we show how the optimization problems (
4
) and (
5
) can be reformulated in order to use the LAR algorithm (cf.
Efron et al. (2004)
). The LAR algorithm is designed to optimize
\|Y-\delta{\mathbf{1}}-X\gamma\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda\|\gamma\|_{1}
in
(\gamma,\delta)\in\mathbb{R}^{q}\times\mathbb{R}
for a given vector
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and a given matrix
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times q}
. Here
{\mathbf{1}}
denotes an
n
-dimensional vector of all
1
’s. We refer to this set-up as the pure least squares linear model. Neither (
4
) nor (
5
) is in this form if we consider all three parameters
(C,\alpha,\theta)
at once. Therefore, we keep two parameters fixed and minimize with respect to the third parameter. For
\theta=(\beta,\gamma)\in\Theta
and
i=1,...,n
, we define matrices
V_{n}(\beta),A_{n}(\gamma),G_{n}(\theta),\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
via

\displaystyle V_{n,ii}(\beta)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{T}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\beta)^{2}dt,\quad V_{n,ij}=0\textrm{ if }i\neq j,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{n,ij}(\gamma)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{-\infty}^{t-}g(t-s;\gamma)dN_{n,i}(s)\cdot\int_{-\infty}^{t-}g(t-r;\gamma)dN_{n,j}(r)dt,

\displaystyle G_{n,ij}(\theta)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{T}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\beta)\int_{-\infty}^{t-}g(t-s;\gamma)dN_{n,j}(s)dt,

\displaystyle A_{n,ij}(\gamma)

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{-\infty}^{t-}g(t-s;\gamma)dN_{n,j}(s)dN_{n,i}(t),

and a vector
v_{n}(\beta)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
via

v_{n,i}(\beta):=\int_{0}^{T}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\beta)dN_{n,i}(t).

Using these definitions we can rewrite

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\Psi_{n,i}(t;c,a,\theta)^{2}dt

\displaystyle=a^{2}V_{n,ii}(\beta)+c\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)c^{T}+2acG_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}(\theta)^{T}\textrm{ and}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\Psi_{n,i}(t;c,a,\theta)dN_{n,i}(t)

\displaystyle=av_{n,i}(\beta)+cA_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}(\gamma)^{T}.

This implies

\displaystyle\textrm{LS}_{i}(C,a,\theta)

\displaystyle=a^{2}V_{n,ii}(\beta)+c\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)c^{T}+2acG_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}(\theta)^{T}-2av_{n,i}(\beta)-2cA_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}(\gamma)^{T},

(14)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\textrm{LS}_{i}(C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\alpha_{i},\theta)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{T}V_{n}(\beta)\alpha+\textrm{tr}\left(C_{n}\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)C_{n}^{T}\right)+2\alpha^{T}\textrm{diag}\left(C_{n}G_{n}(\theta)^{T}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad-2\alpha^{T}v_{n}(\beta)-2\textrm{tr}\left(C_{n}A_{n}(\gamma)^{T}\right),

(15)

where
\textrm{tr}(A)
denotes the trace of the matrix
A
and
\textrm{diag}(A)
the diagonal of the matrix
A
written as column vector. The following result serves as a definition of
\Gamma_{n}^{-1}
in case
\Gamma_{n}
is not invertible.

Lemma A.1
.

The matrix
\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)
is positively semi-definite and symmetric. It can hence be written as
\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)=M\Lambda M^{T}
for a diagonal matrix
\Lambda=\textrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},...,\lambda_{r},0,...,0)
with
\lambda_{1}\geq...\geq\lambda_{r}>0
and
r\leq n
and an orthogonal matrix
M
. Denote,

\Lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}:=\textrm{diag}(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},...,\sqrt{\lambda}_{r},0,...,0)\textrm{ and }\Lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}}:=\textrm{diag}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{1}}},...,\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda}_{r}},0,...,0\right)

as well as
\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)^{\frac{1}{2}}:=M\Lambda^{\frac{1}{2}}M^{T}
and
\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)^{-\frac{1}{2}}:=M\Lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}}M^{T}
. It holds that
\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)^{\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)
.

Proof.

Let
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be arbitrary. Then,

\displaystyle x^{T}\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)x

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{-\infty}^{t-}g(t-r;\gamma)dN_{n,j}(r)x_{j}\right)^{2}dt\geq 0.

The remaining statements are easy to check.
∎

Remark A.2
.

Let
w_{n}(t):=\left(\int_{-\infty}^{t-}g(t-r;\gamma)dN_{n,j}(r)\right)_{j=1,...,n}
. The above proof shows that
\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)
is rank deficient if there is
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
such that
w_{n}(t)^{T}x=0
for all
t\in[0,T]
. A typical reason why this could happen is if for some
i
,
w_{n,i}(t)=0
for all
t\in[0,T]
, i.e., if
N_{n,i}
does not jump at all. Recall that counting process with probability
1
jump at different times. Hence,
w_{n,i}(t)\neq w_{n,j}(t)
for all
i\neq j
and all
t
with probability one if
w_{n,i}(t)\neq 0
and
w_{n,j}(t)\neq 0
. Furthermore, since
w_{n}(t)
changes whenever there is a jump in any of the processes
N_{n,i}
, we regard other situations in which
\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)
is rank deficient as highly unlikely.

The next lemma shows that if some process not jumping is the only reason for rank deficiency of
\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)
, we can rewrite the optimization tasks (
4
) and (
5
) in the form required by the LAR algorithm.

Lemma A.3
.

Assume that
V_{n,ii}(\beta)>0
for all
i=1,...,n
. Suppose without loss of generality that
N_{n,j}
does not jump for
j\in L
with
L=\{n_{0}+1,...,n\}
or
L=\emptyset
. Let
\overline{\Gamma}_{n}(\gamma)
denote the upper left
n_{0}\times n_{0}
block of
\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)
. If
\overline{\Gamma}_{n}(\gamma)
is positive definite, it holds that (below
c\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is a row-vector)

\displaystyle\textrm{LS}_{i}(c,a,\theta)-\left\|\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(A_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}(\gamma)^{T}-aG_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}(\theta)^{T}\right)-\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)^{\frac{1}{2}}c^{T}\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=f(a,\theta),

(16)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\textrm{LS}_{i}(C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\alpha_{i},\theta)-\left\|V_{n}(\beta)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(v_{n}(\beta)-\textrm{diag}(C_{n}G_{n}(\theta)^{T})\right)-V_{n}(\beta)^{\frac{1}{2}}\alpha\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=f(C_{n},\theta),

(17)

where
f(a,\theta)
is a not further specified function of
a
and
\theta
, which is independent of
c
, and similarly
f(C_{n},\theta)
is not a function of
\alpha
.

Proof.

Clearly,
\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)
equals
0
outside the upper left block
\overline{\Gamma}_{n}(\gamma)
. By assumption
\overline{\Gamma}_{n}=WDW^{T}
, for some orthogonal matrix
W\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{0}\times n_{0}}
and
D
a
n_{0}\times n_{0}
diagonal matrix with positive entries. It follows that
M
from Lemma
A.1
is a block-diagonal matrix with
W
in its upper left and a
(n-n_{0})\times(n-n_{0})
identity matrix in the bottom right. Similarly,
\Lambda
equals
D
in the upper right and equals
0
everywhere else. Using these considerations it is direct to compute that
\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)^{\frac{1}{2}}=I_{n_{0},n}
, where
I_{n_{0},n}
is a diagonal matrix with
n_{0}

1
s in the first entries of the diagonal and
n-n_{0}

0
s at the end.

We show the statements now by showing that the corresponding derivatives equal zero: Using the above argument and (
14
),

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dc}\eqref{eq:easyC}

\displaystyle=2\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)c^{T}+2aG_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}(\theta)^{T}-2A_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}(\gamma)^{T}+2\left(I_{n_{0},n}\left(A_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}(\gamma)^{T}-aG_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}(\theta)^{T}\right)-\Gamma_{n}(\gamma)c^{T}\right)

\displaystyle=2a\left(I_{n}-I_{n_{0},n}\right)G_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}(\theta)^{T}-2\left(I_{n}-I_{n_{0},n}\right)A_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}(\gamma)^{T}=0

because
A_{n,ij}(\gamma)=0
for all
j\geq n_{0}+1
and
G_{n,ij}(\theta)=0
for all
j\geq n_{0}+1
.

In a similar fashion, using (
15
), we get

\displaystyle\frac{d}{d\alpha}\eqref{eq:easyNU}

\displaystyle=2V_{n}(\beta)\alpha+2\textrm{diag}(C_{n}G_{n}(\theta)^{T})-2v_{n}(\beta)+2\left(v_{n}(\beta)-\textrm{diag}(C_{n}G_{n}(\theta)^{T})-V_{n}(\beta)\alpha\right)=0.

This completes the proof.
∎

Equation (
16
) shows that optimization in
c
can be understood as a pure least squares linear model with
\delta=0
. Equation (
17
) shows the same about optimization in
\alpha
. Moreover, we can see from (
17
) that finding the baseline parameter
\alpha
is (unsurprisingly) the same problem as regressing constants on the number of events after subtracting the events induced by self-excitement. The equation (
16
) is less easy to interpret. Lastly we note that, if the parameter
\alpha
is left unpenalized (i.e.
\omega_{\alpha}=0
), we can compute the minimizer for fixed
C
and
\theta
by solving
\partial_{\alpha}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\textrm{LS}_{i}(\widehat{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\alpha,\widehat{\theta}_{n})=0
to be
\alpha=V_{n}(\widehat{\beta}_{n})^{-1}\left(v_{n}(\widehat{\beta}_{n})-\textrm{diag}\left(\widehat{C}_{n}G_{n}\left(\widehat{\theta}_{n}\right)^{T}\right)\right)
.

Note finally that, in order to use the LAR algorithm for an efficient computation of the Lasso estimator, we have to either include an intercept
\delta
that remains unpenalized or provide centralized data, cf. Algorithm 5.1 in
Hastie et al. (2015)
. Since neither is the case in (
16
) and (
17
), we need to make a further argument. This will be given in the next subsection.

A.2
Lasso with intercept

Suppose we are interested in solving

\widehat{\gamma}_{n}:=\underset{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}{\operatorname{argmin}}\|Y-X\gamma\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda\|\gamma\|_{1}=\underset{\gamma}{\operatorname{argmin}}\,Y^{T}Y+\gamma^{T}X^{T}X\gamma-2Y^{T}X\gamma+\lambda\|\gamma\|_{1}

(18)

efficiently for general
Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
X\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times p}
. The highly efficient LAR algorithm, cf. Algorithm 5.1 in
Hastie et al. (2015)
, requires that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}=0\textrm{ and }

(19)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{n}X_{ij}=0\textrm{ for each }j=1,...,p.

(20)

We show in this section how LAR can be used in other scenarios as well. Since we believe that this discussion might be of general interest, we discuss this problem generally and therefore the notation in this subsection is independent of the rest of the paper. However, this observation might have been made elsewhere. Typically, if (
19
) and (
20
) do not hold, one centralizes them. This essentially means that one assumes an unpenalized intercept. In our scenario (and possibly in others too), the intercept is a more complicated part of the model (in our case the baseline intensity). Therefore it cannot simply be removed manually and it is also penalized. We describe in this section what to do in such a case. The following result is a preparation for the main statement.

Lemma A.4
.

Let

E:=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{\sqrt{6}}{6}&-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\\
\frac{\sqrt{6}}{6}&\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\end{pmatrix}\textrm{ and }v:=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{\sqrt{6}}{3}&0\end{pmatrix}.

For any even
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\geq 2
define the matrices (all blocks
E
and
v
appear
m/2
times below)

\widetilde{X}_{m}:=\begin{pmatrix}E&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&E\\
v&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&v\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{\frac{3m}{2}\times m}.

For odd
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\geq 2
define (all blocks
E
and
v
appear
(m-1)/2
times below)

\widetilde{X}_{m}:=\begin{pmatrix}E&&&\\
&\ddots&&\\
&&E&\\
&&&\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\\
v&&&\\
&\ddots&&\\
&&v&\\
&&&-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{\frac{3m+1}{2}\times m}.

It holds for all
m\geq 2
that
\widetilde{X}_{m}^{T}\widetilde{X}_{m}=I_{m}
and
\sum_{i}\widetilde{X}_{m,ij}=0
for all
j=1,...,m
, where
I_{m}
denotes the
m\times m
identity matrix.

Proof.

Let
k\in\{1,...,m\}
. Then,

\displaystyle\left[\widetilde{X}_{m}^{T}\widetilde{X}_{m}\right]_{k,k}=\sum_{j}\widetilde{X}_{m,jk}^{2}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\left(\frac{\sqrt{6}}{6}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\sqrt{6}}{6}\right)^{2}+\left(-\frac{\sqrt{6}}{3}\right)^{2}&\textrm{ if }k\textrm{ odd and }k<\frac{3m+1}{2}\\
\left(\frac{-\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)^{2}&\textrm{ if }k\textrm{ even}\\
\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)^{2}+\left(-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)^{2}&\textrm{ if }k=\frac{3m+1}{2}\textrm{ and }m\textrm{ odd}\\
\end{array}\right\}=1

as required. Moreover, for
k\neq l
, we obtain
\left[\widetilde{X}_{m}^{T}\widetilde{X}_{m}\right]_{k,l}=\sum_{j}\widetilde{X}_{m,jk}\widetilde{X}_{m,jl}=0
if
|k-l|\geq 2
. If
k
is even and
l=k+1
, the statement remains true because then the columns
k
and
l
cover different blocks. Similarly, if
k
is odd and
l=k-1
, the statement remains true. Finally, it also holds if
l=m
or
k=m
, when
m
is odd. So we have left to check the situation that
k<m
is odd and
l=k+1
(and the case that
k\leq m
is even and
l=k-1
but this works in the same way)

\displaystyle\left[\widetilde{X}_{m}^{T}\widetilde{X}_{m}\right]_{m,k(k+1)}=\sum_{j}\widetilde{X}_{m,jk}\widetilde{X}_{m,j(k+1)}=-\frac{\sqrt{6}}{6}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}+\frac{\sqrt{6}}{6}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}=0.

This proves
\widetilde{X}_{m}^{T}\widetilde{X}_{m}=I_{m}
. The property
\sum_{i}\widetilde{X}_{m,ij}=0
for
j=1,...,m
is easy to check.

∎

With the help of the above lemma, we prove the main result of this section, which provides the desired representation of (
18
) in which (
19
) and (
20
) hold:

Lemma A.5
.

Let
Y,X,n,p
be as in (
18
) and let
\widetilde{X}_{m}
be defined as in Lemma
A.4
for
m=n
. Define
\overline{Y}:=\widetilde{X}_{m}Y
and
\overline{X}:=\widetilde{X}_{m}X
. Then,
\overline{Y}
and
\overline{X}
fulfill (
19
) and (
20
) and

\overline{\gamma}_{n}:=\underset{\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}{\operatorname{argmin}}\left\|\overline{Y}-\overline{X}\gamma\right\|_{2}^{2}+\lambda\|\gamma\|_{1}

equals
\widehat{\gamma}_{n}
from (
18
).

Proof.

Using Lemma
A.4
, we see that

\displaystyle\left\|\overline{Y}-\overline{X}\gamma\right\|_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\overline{Y}^{T}\overline{Y}+\gamma^{T}\overline{X}^{T}\overline{X}\gamma-2\overline{Y}^{T}\overline{X}\gamma=Y^{T}\widetilde{X}_{m}^{T}\widetilde{X}_{m}Y+\gamma^{T}X^{T}\widetilde{X}_{m}^{T}\widetilde{X}_{m}X\gamma-2Y^{T}\widetilde{X}_{m}^{T}\widetilde{X}_{m}X

\displaystyle=Y^{T}Y+\gamma^{T}X^{T}X\gamma-2Y^{T}X.

Comparing this with (
18
) and since the penalty remains unchanged, it is clear that
\widehat{\gamma}_{n}=\overline{\gamma}_{n}
. It remains to check (
19
) and (
20
). We have by Lemma
A.4
for all
j=1,...,p

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\overline{Y}_{i}=\sum_{i}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\widetilde{X}_{m,ik}Y_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}Y_{k}\sum_{i}\widetilde{X}_{m,ik}=0,

\displaystyle\sum_{i}\overline{X}_{ij}=\sum_{i}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\widetilde{X}_{m,ik}X_{kj}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}X_{kj}\sum_{i}\widetilde{X}_{m,ik}=0,

and the proof is complete.
∎

Lemma
A.5
can now be used to formulate a pure least squares problem which is equivalent to our estimation problem. This least squares problem, in turn, can be solved effectively by using the LAR algorithm.

Appendix B
Useful results about Hawkes processes

B.1
Existence and identifiability of the Hawkes process in our model

Lemma B.1
.

Under Assumption (A1), for any choice of
n\in\mathbb{N}
and parameters
(C,\alpha,\theta)\in\mathcal{H}_{n},
there is a unique multivariate counting process
N_{n}=(N_{n,1},...,N_{n,n})
with intensity functions given by Equation (
1
) with
(C_{n}^{*},\alpha_{n}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*})=(C,\alpha,\theta)
and
N_{n,i}((-\infty,0))=0
. It holds that
\mathbb{E}(N_{n,i}([a,b]))<\infty
for any finite interval
[a,b]
and all
i=1,...,n
. In particular, the processes
M_{n,i}(t):=N_{n,i}(t)-\int_{0}^{t}\lambda_{n,i}(s)ds
are local, square-integrable martingales.

Moreover, the model is identified in the sense that processes with different parameters in Equation (
1
) have different distributions.

The proof of this lemma is using the cluster representation which is as follows (see also
Hawkes and Oakes (1974)
). Consider a multivariate Hawkes process
N=(N_{1},...,N_{n})
with intensity functions

\lambda_{i}(t):=\nu_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\int_{-\infty}^{t-}h_{t,i,j}(t-r)dN_{j}(r)

for a vector
\nu\in[0,\infty)^{n}
and deterministic functions
h_{t,i,j}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
that we allow to depend on
t
. Consider independent counting processes
N_{b,i}
with constant intensities
\nu_{i}
for
i=1,...,n
. Let
\mathcal{W}(0):=(\mathcal{W}_{1}(0),...,\mathcal{W}_{n}(0))
be a vector of given, discrete sets
\mathcal{W}_{i}(0)\subseteq\mathbb{R}
. Having defined the sets
\mathcal{W}(K-1)
the random sets
\mathcal{W}(K)
are defined as follows:

\mathcal{W}_{i}(K):=\bigcup_{j=1}^{n}\bigcup_{t\in\mathcal{W}_{j}(K-1)}\left(t+\{\textrm{jumps of }N_{t,i,j}\}\right),

where in every term of the union the process
N_{t,i,j}
is a different independent copy of a counting process with intensity
h_{t,i,j}(\cdot)
. The sets
\mathcal{W}_{i}(K)
are called events of
i
in generation
K
. It can be shown that if
\mathcal{W}(0)
contains the sets of jump points of the process
N_{b,i}
, then it holds that

N_{i}\sim\bigcup_{K=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{W}_{i}(K),

see
Bacry et al. (2015)
. We now use the cluster representation to prove Lemma
B.1
.

Proof of Lemma
B.1
.

Theorem 7 in
Brémaud and Massoulié (1996)
states that there is a unique multivariate Hawkes process
\overline{N}
(with finite expectations) with intensity given by (
1
), where
\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\beta)
is replaced by
\overline{\nu}_{i}
if the largest eigenvalue of the matrix

A:=C
is strictly less than
1
. This, in turn, holds by the following argument: Suppose that
\mu\in\mathbb{C}
and
v=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{n})\in\mathbb{C}^{n}\setminus\{0\}
are an eigenpair of
C
, i.e.,
Cv=\mu v
. Let
k\in\{1,...,n\}
be such that
|v_{k}|=\|v\|_{\infty}
. Then (since
v\neq 0
,
|v_{k}|>0
),

|\mu|\|v\|_{\infty}=|\mu v_{k}|=\left|\left(Cv\right)_{k}\right|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}C_{k,j}|v_{j}|\leq\|C_{k\cdot}\|_{1}\|v\|_{\infty}\leq\|\nu\|_{\infty},

i.e.
|\mu|<1
.

The process
\overline{N}
has stationary increments and the expected number of events on any finite interval is finite. Note that the stationary process has cluster representation with
h_{t,i,j}(s)=g(s;\gamma)
and
\nu_{i}=\alpha_{0,i}\overline{\nu}_{i}
. From this cluster representation of
\overline{N}
we can easily construct the cluster representation of a process
N_{n}
which has dynamics described by (
1
): In a first step one removes all
t<0
from
\mathcal{W}_{i}(0)
, then, in a second step, since
\alpha_{0,i}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\beta)\leq\nu_{i}
, we can thin the process
N_{b,i}
from the cluster representation such that it has time-varying intensity
\alpha_{0,i}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\beta)
. Then in the construction of
\mathcal{W}_{i}(K)
one removes the sets

\left(t+\{\textrm{jumps of }N_{t,i,j}\}\right)

for those
t
that have been removed from
\mathcal{W}_{j}(K-1)
. Thus, by thinning of the stationary process
\overline{N}
, we obtain a process
N_{n}
with dynamics (
1
). Because
N_{n}
is bounded by
\overline{N}
,
N_{n}
has also finite expectations. Furthermore, because the cluster representation of the process is uniquely defined, cf. discussion after Proposition 6 in
Bacry et al. (2015)
, we have that the process is unique.

For the second part of the statement, let
N_{n}
and
\widetilde{N}_{n}
be two processes with dynamics described in Equation (
1
) for two parameter sets
(C_{n}^{*},\alpha_{n}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*})
and
(C_{n}^{**},\alpha_{n}^{**},\theta_{n}^{**})
, respectively, such that
(C_{n}^{*},\alpha_{n}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*})\neq(C_{n}^{**},\alpha_{n}^{**},\theta_{n}^{**})
.
The assumptions imply that the intensity functions of some of the processes
N_{b,i}
or
N_{t,i,j}
used in the cluster representation of
N_{n}
have a different intensity function of their counterpart in the cluster representation of
\widetilde{N}_{n}
.
Therefore, the cluster representations of
\widetilde{N}
and
\widetilde{N}_{n}
are different.
The above mentioned uniqueness of the cluster representation, in turn, implies that
N_{n}
and
\widetilde{N}_{n}
must have different distributions.
∎

B.2
Bounds on increments of Hawkes processes

In this section, we prove that Hawkes processes have almost uniformly bounded increments (up to a
\log
-factor). Let
N_{n,i}
for
i=1,...,n
be Hawkes processes with dynamics (
1
). For given
A>0
and
\mathcal{N}>0
, let

\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}:=\left\{\forall k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}\cap\left[0,\frac{T}{A}\right]:\,|N_{n,i}\left[kA,(k+1)A\right)|\leq\frac{\mathcal{N}}{2}\textrm{ for all }i\in\{1,...,n\}\right\}.

Lemma B.2
.

Suppose that (A1) holds. There are
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
r>0
such that
|e^{x}-1|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{a_{0}}|x|
for all
|x|\leq r
. Let
a:=r(1-\varepsilon)
. Then, any
\mathcal{N}_{0}>0
, it holds with
\mathcal{N}:=6\mathcal{N}_{0}\cdot\log Tn
, that

\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{N}}^{c}\right)\leq\left(2\exp\left(\frac{A\|\overline{\nu}\|_{\infty}\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\cdot r\varepsilon^{2}}{a_{0}(1-\varepsilon)}\right)+\exp\left(\|\overline{\nu}\|_{\infty}A\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\left(e^{a}-1\right)\right)\right)\frac{T+A}{AT}(nT)^{1-a\mathcal{N}_{0}}.

For the proof of Lemma
B.2
, fix
l\in\{1,...,n\}
, and consider the cluster representation introduced in Section
B.1
with initial sets
\mathcal{W}_{i}(0)
as follows:
\mathcal{W}_{l}(0)
contains exactly one element and
\mathcal{W}_{j}(0)=\emptyset
for
j\neq l
. For
i\in\{1,...,n\}
, denote
W_{i}^{l}(K):=\left|\bigcup_{k=0}^{K}\mathcal{W}_{i}(k)\right|
and
W_{i}^{l}:=W_{i}^{l}(\infty)
. Repeating this construction for each
l\in\{1,...,n\}
yields random vectors
W^{l}(k)=\left(W_{1}^{l}(k),...,W_{n}^{l}(k)\right)\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
for
l=1,..,n
and
k\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{\infty\}
.
We first state and prove a slightly stronger version of Lemma 1 and Proposition 2 in
Hansen et al. (2015)
that fits our setting.

Lemma B.3
.

Let Assumption (A1) hold and let
N_{i},i=1,...,n
be Hawkes processes with intensity functions

\lambda_{n,i}(t):=\alpha^{*}_{n,i}\cdot\overline{\nu}_{i}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}C^{*}_{n,ij}\int_{-\infty}^{t-}g(t-r;\gamma_{n}^{*})dN_{n,j}(r),

where
\overline{\nu}_{i}
is defined in Assumption (A1) and
(C_{n}^{*},\alpha_{n}^{*},\gamma_{n}^{*})=(C,\alpha,\gamma)
arbitrary. Then, with
\varepsilon,r
as in Lemma
B.2
, for all
s\in[0,\infty)^{n}
with
\|s\|_{1}\leq r(1-\varepsilon)
it holds that

\displaystyle\sum_{l=1}^{n}\left|\log\mathbb{E}\left(e^{s^{T}W^{l}}\right)\right|\leq r,

(21)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\left|\mathbb{E}\left(e^{s^{T}(W^{l}-W^{l}(k))}\right)-1\right|\leq\frac{r\varepsilon^{2}}{a_{0}(1-\varepsilon)}.

(22)

The proof takes many ideas of the proofs of Lemma 1 and Proposition 2 in
Hansen et al. (2015)
. However, for our purposes, we need to refine some of the arguments in order to prove a stronger bound on the sum of the expectations. For completeness, we provide the proof in Appendix
F
.

Proof of Lemma
B.2
.

In this proof, we use again ideas from Proposition 2 of
Hansen et al. (2015)
. Note that
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{\mathcal{N}})\geq\mathbb{P}(\overline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{N}})
, where
\overline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{N}}
is defined as
\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}
but for the Hawkes process defined in Lemma
B.3
. By a thinning argument, it is clear that these new processes can be used to form a stationary upper bound of the original processes. In the following, all counting processes are understood to be the stationary upper bounds. By stationarity, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{N}}^{c}\right)\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\lceil\frac{T}{A}\rceil}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}\left(|N_{n,i}[kA,(k+1)A)|>\frac{\mathcal{N}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{T+A}{A}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}\left(|N_{n,i}[-A,0)|>\frac{\mathcal{N}}{2}\right).

(23)

Recall that, in the cluster representation,
N_{b,j}
denotes the
0
-th generation events and that we collected them in
W_{j}(0)
. Consider now such a
0
-th generation event from the interval
[-(k+1)A,-kA)
. Since
g(\cdot;\gamma)
is supported on
[0,A]
, we conclude that any
1
st generation event must have occurred by time
-(k-1)A
. Continuing this argument inductively explains that every
0
-th generation event from the interval
[-(k+1)A,-kA)
can only spawn offspring events in the interval
[-A,0)
from the
k
-th generation onwards. Therefore, the number of events that a
0
-th generation event in process
N_{b,l}
in the interval
[-(k+1)A,-kA)
can produce in the process
N_{n,i}
in the interval
[-A,0)
is upper bounded (in distribution) by
N_{i}^{l}(k):=W_{i}^{l}-W_{i}^{l}(k-1)
for
k\geq 1
and
N_{i}^{l}(0):=W_{i}^{l}
. We abuse notation, and denote by
N_{i}^{l}(k)
a collection of jointly independent random variables with the aforementioned marginal distributions. Denote by
N_{i,m}^{l}(k)
iid copies of
N_{i}^{l}(k)
indexed by
m
. Since every event must be a member of a cluster that eventually originates from a
0
-th generation event, and since the clusters evolve independently, we conclude that for each
x\geq 0

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(|N_{n,i}[-A,0)|\geq x)\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{m=1}^{|N_{b,l}[-(k+1)A,-kA)|}N_{i,m}^{l}(k)\geq x\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{m=1}^{|N_{b,l}[-(k+1)A,-kA)|}N_{i,m}^{l}(k)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{m=1}^{|N_{b,l}[-A,0)|}\left(N_{i,m}^{l}(0)-W_{i}^{l}(0)\right)+|N_{b,i}[-A,0)|\geq x\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{m=1}^{|N_{b,l}[-(k+1)A,-kA)|}N_{i,m}^{l}(k)\geq\frac{x}{3}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{m=1}^{|N_{b,l}[-A,0)|}\left(N_{i,m}^{l}(0)-W_{i}^{l}(0)\right)\geq\frac{x}{3}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\mathbb{P}\left(|N_{b,i}[-A,0)|\geq\frac{x}{3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{m=1}^{|N_{b,l}[-(k+1)A,-kA)|}N_{i,m}^{l}(k)\geq\frac{x}{3}\right)+\mathbb{P}\left(|N_{b,i}[-A,0)|\geq\frac{x}{3}\right)

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{ax}{3}}\left(2\mathbb{E}\left(e^{a\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{m=1}^{|N_{b,l}[-(k+1)A,-kA)|}N_{i,m}^{l}(k)}\right)+\mathbb{E}\left(e^{a|N_{b,i}[-A,0)|}\right)\right)

(24)

The penultimate inequality holds because
N_{i,m}^{l}(0)-W_{i}^{l}(0)
has the same distribution as
N_{i,m}^{l}(1)
, and
N_{b,l}[-(k+1)A,-kA)
are independent. We begin with bounding the first expectation from (
24
). Below, we make use of the independence of the involved random variables and the fact that
N_{b,l}[-(k+1)A,-kA)
is Poisson distributed with rate
A\overline{\nu}_{i}\alpha_{i}

\displaystyle\log\mathbb{E}\left(e^{a\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\sum_{m=1}^{|N_{b,l}[-(k+1)A,-kA)|}N_{i,m}^{l}(k)}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{l=1}^{n}\log\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbb{E}\left(e^{aN_{i,m}^{l}(k)}\right)^{|N_{b,l}[-(k+1)A,-kA)|}\right)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\sum_{l=1}^{n}A\overline{\nu}_{i}\alpha_{i}\left(\mathbb{E}\left(e^{aN_{i,m}^{l}(k)}\right)-1\right)

\displaystyle\leq A\|\overline{\nu}\|_{\infty}\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\frac{r\varepsilon^{2}}{a_{0}(1-\varepsilon)},

by (
22
) of Lemma
B.3
, which we apply here with
s\in[0,\infty)^{n}
with
s_{i}=a=r(1-\varepsilon)
and
s_{j}=0
for
j\neq i
because
N_{i,m}^{l}(k)\sim W_{i}^{l}-W_{i}^{l}(k-1)
. Since,
|N_{b,i}[-A,0)|
follows a Poisson distribution with parameter
A\overline{\nu}_{i}\alpha_{i}
, we get for the second expectation in (
24
) that

\log\mathbb{E}\left(e^{a|N_{b,i}[-A,0)|}\right)=A\alpha_{i}\overline{\nu}_{i}\left(e^{a}-1\right)\leq A\|\overline{\nu}\|_{\infty}\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\left(e^{a}-1\right).

Using the previous two displays in (
24
), we find

\mathbb{P}(|N_{n,i}[-A,0)|\geq x)\leq e^{-\frac{ax}{3}}\left(2\exp\left(\frac{A\|\overline{\nu}\|_{\infty}\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\cdot r\varepsilon^{2}}{a_{0}(1-\varepsilon)}\right)+\exp\left(A\|\overline{\nu}\|_{\infty}\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\left(e^{a}-1\right)\right)\right).

(25)

Plugging the above with
x=\mathcal{N}/2
in (
23
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\overline{\Omega}_{\mathcal{N}}^{c}\right)\leq

\displaystyle\frac{T+A}{A}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(e^{-\frac{a\mathcal{N}}{6}}\left(2\exp\left(A\|\overline{\nu}\|_{\infty}\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\frac{r\varepsilon^{2}}{a_{0}(1-\varepsilon)}\right)+\exp\left(A\|\overline{\nu}\|_{\infty}\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\left(e^{a}-1\right)\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(2\exp\left(A\|\overline{\nu}\|_{\infty}\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\frac{r\varepsilon^{2}}{a_{0}(1-\varepsilon)}\right)+\exp\left(A\|\overline{\nu}\|_{\infty}\|\alpha\|_{\infty}\left(e^{a}-1\right)\right)\right)\frac{T+A}{AT}(nT)^{1-a\mathcal{N}_{0}}.

The proof is complete.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs

C.1
Proofs of Section
3.1

The proof of Theorem
3.2
is given in the Appendix. The proof of Proposition
3.3
is based on Lemma
3.5
, which in turn follows from Lemma
C.1
that we formulate and prove first:

Lemma C.1
.

Suppose that (A1),(A2), and (A3) hold. Let
\mathcal{N}_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3},\alpha_{4}>0
be arbitrary, and let
\mu\in(0,3)
be such that
\mu>\phi(\mu),
where
\phi(u)=e^{u}-u-1
. Denote

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{a,\mathcal{T}}^{\mu}:

\displaystyle=\underset{\overline{\beta}\in K_{\beta}}{\sup_{i=1,...,n}}\frac{16\mu\int_{0}^{T}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\overline{\beta})^{2}dN_{n,i}(t)}{(\mu-\phi(\mu))T^{2}}+\frac{16\|\overline{\nu}\|_{\infty}^{2}\left(\log(n)+p\log T+\alpha_{1}\log(nT)\right)}{(\mu-\phi(\mu))T^{2}},

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{b,\mathcal{T}}^{\mu}:

\displaystyle=\underset{\beta_{2}:\|\beta_{2}\|_{1}=1}{\sup_{\beta_{1}\in K_{\beta}}}\frac{16K_{\alpha}^{2}\mu\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{d\beta}\nu_{0}\left(X_{n,i}(t);(1-s)\beta_{n}^{*}+s\beta_{1}\right)^{T}\beta_{2}ds\right)^{2}dN_{n,i}(t)}{(\mu-\phi(\mu))n^{2}T^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{16K_{\alpha}^{2}L_{\nu}^{2}(2p+\alpha_{2})\log(nT)}{(\mu-\phi(\mu))n^{2}T^{2}},

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{d,\mathcal{T},i}^{\mu}:

\displaystyle=\underset{\overline{\gamma}\in K_{\gamma}}{\sup_{j\in\{1,...,n\}}}\frac{16\mu\int_{0}^{T}\left(\int_{0}^{t-}g(t-r;\overline{\gamma})dN_{n,j}(r)\right)^{2}dN_{n,i}(t)}{(\mu-\phi(\mu))T^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{567\overline{g}^{2}\mathcal{N}_{0}^{2}\log^{2}(nT)\cdot\left(\log n+\log(nT)+\alpha_{3}\log T\right)}{(\mu-\phi(\mu))T^{2}},

\displaystyle\widehat{V}_{e,\mathcal{T}}^{\mu}:

\displaystyle=\sup_{\overline{\gamma}\in K_{\gamma}}\frac{\mu}{\mu-\phi(\mu)}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}\Bigg(\sum_{j=1}^{n}C_{n,ij}^{*}\frac{4\int\limits_{0}^{t-}\int\limits_{0}^{1}\frac{d}{d\gamma}g(t-r;s\overline{\gamma}+(1-s)\gamma_{n}^{*})dsdN_{n,j}(r)}{nT}\Bigg)^{2}dN_{n,i}(t)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{576L_{g}^{2}\mathcal{N}_{0}^{2}(1+\alpha_{4})c_{n}^{2}\log^{3}(nT)}{(\mu-\phi(\mu))n^{2}T^{2}}.

Let
\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}
by as in Lemma
B.2
with
\mathcal{N}:=6\mathcal{N}_{0}\log(nT)
where
{\cal N}_{0}
is arbitrary, and denote

\displaystyle a_{n}:

\displaystyle=\Bigg(2\sqrt{\widehat{V}_{a,\mathcal{T}}^{\mu}\left(\log n+p\log T+\alpha_{1}\log(nT)\right)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{4\|\overline{\nu}\|_{\infty}\left(\log n+p\log T+\alpha_{1}\log(nT)\right)}{3T}\Bigg)\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}},

\displaystyle b_{n}:

\displaystyle=\left(2\sqrt{\widehat{V}_{b,\mathcal{T}}^{\mu}(2p+\alpha_{2})\log(nT)}+\frac{4K_{\alpha}L_{\nu}(2p+\alpha_{2})\log(nT)}{3nT}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}},

\displaystyle d_{n,i}:

\displaystyle=\Bigg(2\sqrt{\widehat{V}_{d,\mathcal{T},i}^{\mu}\left(\log n+\log(nT)+\alpha_{3}\log T\right)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{24\overline{g}\mathcal{N}_{0}\log(nT)\cdot\left(\log n+\log(nT)+\alpha_{3}\log T\right)}{3T}\Bigg)\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}},

\displaystyle e_{n}:

\displaystyle=\left(2\sqrt{\widehat{V}_{e,\mathcal{T}}^{\mu}(1+\alpha_{4})\log(nT)}+\frac{24L_{g}\mathcal{N}_{0}(1+\alpha_{4})c_{n}\log^{2}(nT)}{3nT}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}}.

Then, there are constants
a,c_{\Omega},c_{1},c_{2},c_{3},c_{4}>0
, where
a
depends only on
a_{0}
from (A1), such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{n}(a_{n},b_{n},d_{n},e_{n})^{c})

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\frac{c_{n}^{p}\log T}{(nT)^{\alpha_{1}}}+c_{2}\frac{c_{n}^{2p}\log(nT)}{(nT)^{\alpha_{2}}}+c_{3}\frac{c_{n}\log(T)}{T^{\alpha_{3}}}

\displaystyle\qquad+c_{4}\frac{\log(nT)(1+c_{n})}{(nT)^{\alpha_{4}}}+4c_{\Omega}(Tn)^{1-a\mathcal{N}_{0}}.

(26)

Proof of Lemma
C.1
.

The main tool for the proof is the exponential inequality from Theorem 3 in
Hansen et al. (2015)
, and we use ideas from the proofs of Theorem 2 and Proposition 2 in
Hansen et al. (2015)
. Note that for any
t\in[0,T]
the interval
[t,t+A)
can be covered by two intervals of the form
[kA,(k+1)A)
. Then, we have on
\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}
that
N_{n,i}[t,t+A)\leq\mathcal{N}
for all
t\in[0,T]
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Thus, we conclude that on
\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}
for all
i,j=1,...,n

\displaystyle\sup_{\overline{\beta}\in K_{\beta}}\int_{0}^{T}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\overline{\beta})^{2}dN_{n,i}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{T}{A}+1\right)\overline{\nu}_{i}^{2}\mathcal{N},

(27)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\sup_{\overline{\gamma}\in K_{\gamma}}\left|\int_{0}^{t-}g(t-r;\overline{\gamma})dN_{n,j}(r)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\overline{g}\mathcal{N}.

(28)

We fix now
\mathcal{N}:=6\mathcal{N}_{0}\cdot\log(Tn)
and study the four parts of
\mathcal{T}_{n}
separately. In each part, a term
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}^{c})
will appear. This term is always bounded using Lemma
B.2
, which we may apply by Assumptions (A1) and (A2) for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
with the same choice of
a_{0},r,\varepsilon
, yielding the last term in (
26
). Define furthermore the stopping time

\tau_{n}:=T\wedge\inf\left\{t\in[0,\infty):\exists i,j\in\{1,...,n\},\sup_{\overline{\gamma}\in K_{\gamma}}\left|\int_{0}^{t-}g(t-r;\overline{\gamma})dN_{n,j}(r)\right|>\overline{g}\mathcal{N}\right\}.

we have that
\tau_{n}=T
on
\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}
.

Part involving
a_{n}
: We can use a classical
chaining light
argument as follows. Let
K_{\beta,n,\eta}\subseteq K_{\beta}
be finite such that for any
\overline{\beta}\in K_{\beta}
there exists
P_{n}(\overline{\beta})\in K_{\beta,n,\eta}
such that
\|\overline{\beta}-P_{n}(\overline{\beta})\|\leq\eta
. It is possible to choose
K_{\beta,n,\eta}
such that
|K_{\beta,n,\eta}|\leq K_{0}\eta^{-p}
for some constant
K_{0}>0
. Define a constant
c_{1}^{\prime\prime}
such that for all
n
and
T

2L_{\nu}\left(\frac{\mathcal{N}(T+A)}{TA}+K_{\alpha}\|\overline{\nu}\|_{\infty}+c_{n}\overline{g}\mathcal{N}\right)\leq c_{1}^{\prime\prime}c_{n}\log(nT).

This definition is, at this point, unmotivated. Later in the proof, it will turn out that, with
c_{1}^{\prime\prime}
as above, the following choice of
\eta>0
is the right one for our purposes

\eta:=\frac{4\|\overline{\nu}\|_{\infty}\left(\log n+p\log T+\alpha_{1}\log(nT)\right)}{c_{1}^{\prime\prime}\sqrt{\mu-\phi(\mu)}Tc_{n}\log(nT)}.

These definitions will be important later. We first note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{i=1,...,n}\sup_{\overline{\beta}\in K_{\beta}}\frac{2}{T}\left|\int_{0}^{T}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\overline{\beta})dM_{n,i}(t)\right|\geq a_{n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{i=1,...,n}\sup_{\overline{\beta}\in K_{\beta}}\frac{2}{T}\left|\int_{0}^{T}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\overline{\beta})dM_{n,i}(t)\right|\geq a_{n},\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}\right)+\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\sup_{i=1,...,n}\sup_{\overline{\beta}\in K_{\beta}}\Bigg|\frac{2}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\overline{\beta})dM_{n,i}(t)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\frac{2}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);P_{n}(\overline{\beta}))dM_{n,i}(t)\Bigg|\geq\frac{a_{n}}{2},\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}\Bigg)

(29)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{i=1,...,n}\sup_{\overline{\beta}\in K_{\beta,n,\eta}}\frac{2}{T}\left|\int_{0}^{T}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\overline{\beta})dM_{n,i}(t)\right|\geq\frac{a_{n}}{2},\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}\right)+\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}^{c}).

(30)

The probability in (
30
) can be bounded from above using union bound:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{i=1,...,n}\sup_{\overline{\beta}\in K_{\beta,n,\eta}}\frac{2}{T}\left|\int_{0}^{T}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\overline{\beta})dM_{n,i}(t)\right|\geq\frac{a_{n}}{2},\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq nK_{0}\eta^{-p}\sup_{i=1,...,n}\sup_{\overline{\beta}\in K_{\beta,n,\eta}}\mathbb{P}\left(\frac{2}{T}\left|\int_{0}^{T}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\overline{\beta})dM_{n,i}(t)\right|\geq\frac{a_{n}}{2},\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}\right).

(31)

We handle this term by applying Theorem 3 of
Hansen et al. (2015)
for a uni-variate process, i.e., in the notation of
Hansen et al. (2015)
,
M=1
. We keep the notation as in the theorem for the convenience of the reader. We have
H(t)=\frac{4}{T}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\overline{\beta})
, which is uniformly bounded in absolute value by
B:=\frac{4\overline{\nu}_{i}}{T}
. The integral conditions are hence true and we consider the constant stopping time
\tau=T
. We furthermore define for any
x>0

\widehat{V}^{\mu}_{a}:=\frac{\mu}{\mu-\phi(\mu)}\int_{0}^{T}\frac{16\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\overline{\beta})^{2}}{T^{2}}dN_{n,i}(t)+\frac{x}{\mu-\phi(\mu)}\cdot\frac{16\overline{\nu}_{i}^{2}}{T^{2}}.

On
\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}
, it holds by (
27
) that

w:=\frac{16x\overline{\nu}_{i}^{2}}{T^{2}(\mu-\phi(\mu))}\leq\widehat{V}^{\mu}_{a}\leq\frac{16\mu\overline{\nu}_{i}^{2}\mathcal{N}(T+A)}{T^{2}A(\mu-\phi(\mu))}+\frac{16x\overline{\nu}_{i}^{2}}{T^{2}(\mu-\phi(\mu))}=:v.

We hence obtain from Theorem 3 of
Hansen et al. (2015)
for
\varepsilon=1
that, by letting
x=\log n+p\log T+\alpha_{1}\log(nT)
, that there exists
c_{1}^{\prime}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\int_{0}^{T}\frac{2}{T}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\overline{\beta})dM_{n,i}(t)\geq\frac{a_{n}}{2},\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\Bigg(\int_{0}^{T}\frac{4}{T}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\overline{\beta})dM_{n,i}(t)\geq 2\sqrt{\widehat{V}^{\mu}_{a}x}+\frac{Bx}{3},w\leq\widehat{V}_{a}^{\mu}\leq v,

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\sup_{t\in[0,\tau]}\frac{4\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\overline{\beta})}{T}\leq\frac{4\overline{\nu}_{i}}{T}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\log_{2}\left(\frac{v}{w}\right)+1\right)e^{-x}=2\left(\log_{2}\left(1+\frac{\mu\mathcal{N}(T+A)}{Ax}\right)+1\right)e^{-x}\leq c_{1}^{\prime}\log T\cdot n^{-1}T^{-p}(nT)^{-\alpha_{1}},

where we used
\mathcal{N}=6\mathcal{N}_{0}\log(nT)
in the last inequality. Combining the above with (
31
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{i=1,...,n}\sup_{\overline{\beta}\in K_{\beta,n,\eta}}\frac{2}{T}\left|\int_{0}^{T}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\overline{\beta})dM_{n,i}(t)\right|\geq\frac{a_{n}}{2},\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq nK_{0}\eta^{-p}2c_{1}^{\prime}\log T\cdot n^{-1}T^{-p}(nT)^{-\alpha_{1}}

\displaystyle\leq nK_{0}2c_{1}^{\prime}\log T\cdot n^{-1}T^{-p}(nT)^{-\alpha_{1}}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\frac{(c_{1}^{\prime\prime})^{p}(\mu-\phi(\mu))^{\frac{p}{2}}T^{p}c_{n}^{p}\log(nT)^{p}}{4^{p}\|\overline{\nu}\|_{\infty}^{p}\left(\log n+p\log T+\alpha_{1}\log(nT)\right)^{p}}\leq c_{1}\frac{c_{n}^{p}\log T}{(nT)^{\alpha_{1}}}

for a suitable choice of
c_{1}>0
.

We turn now to (
29
). For ease of notation, we denote below
d|M_{n,i}|(t):=dN_{n,i}(t)+\lambda_{n,i}(t)dt
. On the event
\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}
, the Lipschitz continuity of
\nu_{0}
(expressed as differentiability and bounded derivative in (A3)) together with the properties of
N_{n,i}
on
\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}
implies

\displaystyle\sup_{i=1,...,n}\sup_{\overline{\beta}\in K_{\beta}}\left|\frac{2}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\overline{\beta})dM_{n,i}(t)-\frac{2}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);P_{n}(\overline{\beta}))dM_{n,i}(t)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i=1,...,n}\sup_{\overline{\beta}\in K_{\beta}}\frac{2}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\left|\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\overline{\beta})-\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);P_{n}(\overline{\beta}))\right|d|M_{n,i}|(t)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i=1,...,n}\frac{2L_{\nu}\eta}{T}\Bigg(\int_{0}^{T}dN_{n,i}(t)+\int_{0}^{T}\alpha_{n,i}^{*}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\beta_{n}^{*})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{j=1}^{n}C_{n,ij}^{*}\int_{0}^{t-}g(t-r;\gamma_{n}^{*})dN_{n,j}(r)dt\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{i=1,...,n}\frac{2L_{\nu}\eta}{T}\left(\frac{\mathcal{N}(T+A)}{A}+TK_{\alpha}\overline{\nu}_{i}+T\|C_{n,i\cdot}^{*}\|_{1}\overline{g}\mathcal{N}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2L_{\nu}\left(\frac{\mathcal{N}(T+A)}{AT}+K_{\alpha}\|\overline{\nu}\|_{\infty}+c_{n}\overline{g}\mathcal{N}\right)\eta\leq c_{1}^{\prime\prime}c_{n}\log(nT)\eta,

where
c_{1}^{\prime\prime}
was chosen such that the last inequality holds. By choice of
\eta
, we obtain on
\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}

c_{1}^{\prime\prime}c_{n}\log(nT)\eta\leq\sqrt{\sup_{i=1,..,n}wx}\leq\sqrt{\widehat{V}_{a,\mathcal{T}}^{\mu}x}\leq\frac{a_{n}}{2}.

This, finally, implies
\eqref{eq:an_cont}=0
.

The arguments for the remaining parts work similarly, and we defer the details to Appendix
G.1

.
∎

Proof of Lemma
3.5
.

Choose
\mathcal{N}_{0}
such that
a\mathcal{N}_{0}>1
(
a
as in Lemma
B.2
). Then, Lemma
B.2
implies
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{\mathcal{N}})\to 1
for
\mathcal{N}:=6\mathcal{N}_{0}\log(nT)
. Note furthermore that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}_{n}(a_{n},b_{n},d_{n},e_{n}))
increases if we increase
a_{n},b_{n},d_{n},e_{n}
. Therefore, it is sufficient to find deterministic upper bounds on the sequences
a_{n},b_{n},d_{n,i},e_{n}
provided by Lemma
C.1
. Due to the formulas given there, we may restrict to the case when
\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}
holds true. Below,
K
is a constant that may change from appearance to appearance, but it never depends on quantities that change with
n
. Using the bounds on the
\widehat{V}^{\mu}
expressions from the proof of Lemma
C.1
, we obtain

\displaystyle a_{n}

\displaystyle\leq K\left(\sqrt{\left(\frac{\log(nT)}{T}+\frac{\log(nT)}{T^{2}}\right)\log(nT)}+\frac{\log(nT)}{T}\right)\leq K\frac{\log(nT)}{\sqrt{T}},

\displaystyle b_{n}

\displaystyle\leq K\left(\sqrt{\left(\frac{\log(nT)}{nT}+\frac{\log(nT)}{n^{2}T^{2}}\right)\log(nT)}+\frac{\log(nT)}{nT}\right)\leq K\frac{\log(nT)}{\sqrt{nT}},

\displaystyle d_{n,i}

\displaystyle\leq K\left(\sqrt{\left(\frac{\log^{3}(nT)}{T}+\frac{\log^{3}(nT)}{T^{2}}\right)\log(nT)}+\frac{\log^{2}(nT)}{T}\right)\leq K\frac{\log^{2}(nT)}{\sqrt{T}},

\displaystyle e_{n}

\displaystyle\leq K\Bigg(\sqrt{\left(\frac{\log^{3}(nT)c_{n}^{2}}{nT}+\frac{\log^{3}(nT)c_{n}^{2}}{n^{2}T^{2}}\right)\log(nT)}+\frac{c_{n}\log^{2}(nT)}{nT}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq K\frac{\log^{2}(nT)\cdot c_{n}}{\sqrt{nT}}.

Note that, importantly, the constant
K
for bounding
d_{n,i}
does not depend on
i
. These bounds imply the statement since
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{\mathcal{N}})\to 1
by Lemma
B.2
.
∎

Now we are ready to present the proof of Proposition
3.3
.

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

In the situation of Lemma
3.5
, the requirements of Theorem
3.2
are fulfilled if, for a suitable
K_{\widetilde{b}}>0
, we choose

\displaystyle\widetilde{b}_{n}

\displaystyle=K_{\widetilde{b}}\frac{\log^{2}(nT)c_{n}}{\sqrt{T}}\geq\frac{\max\left(K_{b}\log(nT),K_{e}\log^{2}(nT)c_{n}\right)}{\sqrt{nT}},

\displaystyle\omega_{i}

\displaystyle=K_{d}\frac{\log^{2}(nT)}{\sqrt{T}}.

Moreover, using these definitions and the statements of Lemma
3.5
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(C_{n}^{*},\alpha_{n}^{*})^{2}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\frac{\log^{2}(nT)}{T}+\frac{\log^{4}(nT)}{T}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|+\frac{\log^{4}(nT)}{T}c_{n}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\frac{\log^{4}(nT)}{T}\left(1+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|+c_{n}^{2}\right)\right)=O_{P}\Big(\frac{\log^{4}(nT)}{T}s_{n}\Big).

Using the statement of Theorem
3.2
, we conclude if
\phi_{\textrm{comp}}(S_{1}(C_{n}^{*}),...,S_{n}(C_{n}^{*});L;\mathcal{H}_{n})
is uniformly bounded from below,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\left\|\widecheck{C}_{n}-C_{n}^{*}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\frac{\log^{2}(nT)}{\sqrt{T}}s_{n}\right),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\left\|\widecheck{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{*}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\frac{\log^{3}(nT)}{\sqrt{T}}s_{n}\right),

\displaystyle\left\|\widecheck{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\Bigg(\frac{\log^{2}(nT)}{\sqrt{T}}\frac{s_{n}}{c_{n}}\Bigg).

∎

C.2
Proofs of Section
3.2

Let,

J:=\begin{pmatrix}1&&&0&\cdots&0\\
&\ddots&&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&1&0&\cdots&0\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{(p+1)\times N}

denote the first
p+1
rows of
I_{N}
(the identity matrix). We continue by listing the derivatives of
\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\alpha_{i},\theta)
for
i,k,x,y\in\{1,...,n\}

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha_{k}}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\alpha_{i},\theta)

\displaystyle=\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\beta)\mathbbm{1}(i=k),

\displaystyle\partial_{C_{xy}}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\alpha_{i},\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t-}g(t-r;\gamma)dN_{n,y}(r)\mathbbm{1}(i=x),

\displaystyle\partial_{\beta}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\alpha_{i},\theta)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{i}\nu_{0}^{\prime}(X_{n,i}(t);\beta),

\displaystyle\partial_{\gamma}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\alpha_{i},\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}C_{ij}\int_{0}^{t-}g^{\prime}(t-r;\gamma)dN_{n,j}(r),

where
\nu_{0}^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
denote the first derivatices of
\nu_{0}
resp.
g
with respect to
\beta
resp.
\gamma
. Many second order derivatives equal zero. We list here only the non-zero derivatives for
i,k,x,y\in\{1,...,n\}

\displaystyle\partial_{\beta}\partial_{\alpha_{k}}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\alpha_{i},\theta)

\displaystyle=\nu_{0}^{\prime}(X_{n,i}(t);\beta)\mathbbm{1}(i=k),

\displaystyle\partial_{\gamma}\partial_{C_{xy}}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\alpha_{i},\theta)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t-}g^{\prime}(t-r);\gamma)dN_{n,y(r)}\mathbbm{1}(i=x),

\displaystyle\partial_{\beta}\partial_{\beta}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\alpha_{i},\theta)

\displaystyle=\alpha_{0}\nu_{0}^{\prime\prime}(X_{n,i}(t);\beta),

\displaystyle\partial_{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\alpha_{i},\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}C_{ij}\int_{0}^{t-}g^{\prime\prime}(t-r;\gamma)dN_{n,j}(r),

where
\nu_{0}^{\prime\prime}
and
g^{\prime\prime}
denote the second derivatives of
\nu_{0}
resp.
g
with respect to
\beta
resp.
\gamma
.

We present now two technical lemmas, which we prove in Appendix
H

.

Lemma C.2
.

Let (A1) - (A3), and (B1) hold. Let
(C_{1},\alpha_{1},\theta_{1}),(C_{2},\alpha_{2},\theta_{2})\in{\cal H}_{n}
be arbitrary vectors of (random) parameters. Let
R_{n}
be an
N\times N
matrix such that each row
R_{n,a\boldsymbol{\cdot}}
of
R_{n}
equals a row of
\Sigma_{n}(C,\alpha,\theta)
for some
(C,\alpha,\theta)\in{\cal H}_{n}
that can vary with
a
and satisfies
(C_{1},\alpha_{1},\theta_{1})\leq(C,\alpha,\theta)\leq(C_{2},\alpha_{2},\theta_{2})
. Then, if
\frac{\log(nT)}{n}=o(1)
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\max_{a,b}\left|R_{n,ab}-\Sigma_{n,ab}(C_{1},\alpha_{1},\theta_{1})\right|}{\frac{1}{n}\|\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}\|_{1}+\frac{1}{n}\|C_{1}-C_{2}\|_{1}+\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|C_{1,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}\|_{1}+1\right)\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|_{1}}

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt=O_{P}\left(\max_{i=1,...,n}(\|C_{1,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}\|_{1},\|C_{2,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}\|_{1},1)\log^{2}(nT)\right).

Lemma C.3
.

Let (A1), (A2) and (A3) hold, and suppose that
\log(nT)/\sqrt{nT}\to 0
. Then (with
\vee
denoting maximum),

\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\theta}\\
\partial_{\alpha}\\
\partial_{C}\end{pmatrix}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{n,i\cdot}^{*},\alpha_{n,i}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*})dM_{n,i}(t)\right\|_{\infty}=O_{P}\left(c_{n}\log nT\right).

Proof of Theorem
3.6
.

We use the KKT conditions
(
Bertsekas, 1995
, e.g. see)
, to characterize
(\widecheck{C}_{n},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n},\widecheck{\theta}_{n})
. More precisely, define the Lagrange function

\displaystyle L:\mathcal{H}_{n}\times[0,\infty)^{n^{2}+n}\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle((C,\alpha,\theta),(\mu_{C},\mu_{\alpha}))\mapsto\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\frac{1}{T}\textrm{LS}_{i}(C_{i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\alpha_{i},\theta)+2\omega_{i}\|C_{i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}\|_{1}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\mu_{C,ij}(-C_{ij})+\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu_{\alpha,i}(-\alpha_{i}).

Since
\Theta
is an open set and since the inequality constraint in
\mathcal{H}_{n}
is a strict inequality, only the non-negativity constraints are potentially binding. Moreover, the KKT conditions imply the existence of
(\mu_{C},\mu_{\alpha})
with

L((C,\alpha,\theta),(\mu_{C},\mu_{\alpha}))\geq L((\widecheck{C}_{n},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n},\widecheck{\theta}_{n}),(\mu_{C},\mu_{\alpha}))

for all
(C,\alpha,\theta)
which fulfill all constraints of
\mathcal{H}_{n}
other than potentially the non-negativity. Since this is an open set, we may conclude that

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\theta}\\
\partial_{\alpha}\\
\partial_{C}\end{pmatrix}\textrm{LS}_{i}(\widecheck{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n,i},\widecheck{\theta}_{n})+\frac{1}{n}\begin{pmatrix}0_{p+1}\\
0_{n}\\
2\omega_{1}1_{n}\\
\vdots\\
2\omega_{n}1_{n}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}0_{p+1}\\
-\mu_{\alpha}\\
-\mu_{C}\end{pmatrix},

(32)

where
0_{p}
and
1_{p}
are vectors of zeros or ones of size
p
, and
\mu_{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{n^{2}}
is the vector

(\mu_{C,11},...,\mu_{C,1n},\mu_{C,21},...,\mu_{2n},...,\mu_{C,n1},...,\mu_{C,nn}).

Moreover,
\mu_{C,ij}\widecheck{C}_{n,ij}=0
and
\mu_{\alpha,i}\widecheck{\alpha}_{n,i}=0
for all
i,j=1,...,n
. We may rearrange (
32
) to obtain

\begin{pmatrix}0_{p+1}\\
-\mu_{\alpha}\\
\frac{2\omega_{1}}{n}1_{n}-\mu_{C,1\boldsymbol{\cdot}}\\
\vdots\\
\frac{2\omega_{n}}{n}1_{n}-\mu_{C,n\boldsymbol{\cdot}}\end{pmatrix}=-\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\theta}\\
\partial_{\alpha}\\
\partial_{C}\end{pmatrix}\textrm{LS}_{i}(\widecheck{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n,i},\widecheck{\theta}_{n}).

(33)

Recall that
(C_{n}^{*},\alpha_{n}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*})
denotes the true parameters. We then have using a Taylor expansion,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\theta}\\
\partial_{\alpha}\\
\partial_{C}\end{pmatrix}\textrm{LS}_{i}(\widecheck{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n,i},\widecheck{\theta}_{n})

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\theta}\\
\partial_{\alpha}\\
\partial_{C}\end{pmatrix}\textrm{LS}_{i}(C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*},\alpha_{n,i}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*})}_{=:W_{n}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\theta}\\
\partial_{\alpha}\\
\partial_{C}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\theta}\\
\partial_{\alpha}\\
\partial_{C}\end{pmatrix}^{\top}\textrm{LS}_{i}(\widecheck{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n,i},\widecheck{\theta}_{n})}_{=\,\Sigma_{n}(\widecheck{C}_{n},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n},\widecheck{\theta}_{n})}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\widecheck{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{C}_{n}-C_{n}^{*}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(R_{n}-\Sigma_{n}(\widecheck{C}_{n},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n},\widecheck{\theta}_{n})\right)\begin{pmatrix}\widecheck{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{C}_{n}-C_{n}^{*}\end{pmatrix},

where
R_{n}
is a matrix obtained from
\Sigma_{n}
by replacing
\widecheck{C}_{n},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n},\widecheck{\theta}_{n}
in each row by a different intermediate point. Putting the above in (
32
) and multiplying with a (for now) arbitrary matrix
\Lambda_{n}
yields

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=-\Lambda_{n}W_{n}-\Lambda_{n}\Sigma_{n}(\widecheck{C}_{n},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n},\widecheck{\theta}_{n})\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\widecheck{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{C}_{n}-C_{n}^{*}\end{pmatrix}-\Lambda_{n}\left(R_{n}-\Sigma_{n}(\widecheck{C}_{n},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n},\widecheck{\theta}_{n})\right)\begin{pmatrix}\widecheck{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{C}_{n}-C_{n}^{*}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\qquad-\Lambda_{n}\begin{pmatrix}0_{p+1}\\
-\mu_{\alpha}\\
\frac{2\omega_{1}}{n}1_{n}-\mu_{C,1\boldsymbol{\cdot}}\\
\vdots\\
\frac{2\omega_{n}}{n}1_{n}-\mu_{C,n\boldsymbol{\cdot}}\end{pmatrix}.

Adding
(\widecheck{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{*},\widecheck{C}_{n}-C_{n}^{*})^{\top}
on both sides, using (
33
) and the definition of the debiased Lasso estimator
\overline{\theta}_{n}
given in (
7
) and multiplying by
\sqrt{nT}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sqrt{nT}

\displaystyle\left(\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{nT}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\widecheck{\theta}_{n}\\
\widecheck{\alpha}_{n}\\
\widecheck{C}_{n}\end{pmatrix}-{nT}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Lambda_{n}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\theta}\\
\partial_{\alpha}\\
\partial_{C}\end{pmatrix}\textrm{LS}_{i}(\widecheck{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n,i},\widecheck{\theta}_{n})-\begin{pmatrix}\theta_{n}^{*}\\
\alpha_{n}^{*}\\
C_{n}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{nT}\Lambda_{n}W_{n}+\sqrt{nT}\left(I_{N}-\Lambda_{n}\Sigma_{n}(\widecheck{C}_{n},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n},\widecheck{\theta}_{n})\right)\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\widecheck{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{C}_{n}-C_{n}^{*}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad-\sqrt{nT}\Lambda_{n}\left(R_{n}-\Sigma_{n}(\widecheck{C}_{n},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n},\widecheck{\theta}_{n})\right)\begin{pmatrix}\widecheck{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{C}_{n}-C_{n}^{*}\end{pmatrix}.

(34)

We firstly argue that the last two terms converge to zero. We begin with the second term. Note that each row of
R_{n}
contains the remainder term in a first-order Taylor expansion. Therefore, for each row
j
of
R_{n}
, there are
\widetilde{C}_{n}^{(j)}
,
\widetilde{\alpha}_{n}^{(j)}
,
\widetilde{\theta}_{n}^{(j)}
which lie on the connecting lines between
C_{n}^{*}
and
\widecheck{C}_{n}
,
\alpha_{n}^{*}
and
\widecheck{\alpha}_{n}
,
\theta_{n}^{*}
and
\widecheck{\theta}_{n}
, respectively, such that

R_{n,j\cdot}=\Sigma_{n,j\cdot}\left(\widetilde{C}^{(j)}_{n},\widetilde{\alpha}^{(j)}_{n},\widetilde{\theta}^{(j)}_{n}\right).

Then,
R_{n}
is as in Lemma
C.2
. Therefore, Lemma
C.2
implies that

\displaystyle\left\|\left(R_{n}-\Sigma_{n}\left(\widecheck{C}_{n},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n},\widecheck{\theta}_{n}\right)\right)\begin{pmatrix}\widecheck{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{C}_{n}-C_{n}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}\leq\max_{a,b}\left|R_{n,ab}-\Sigma_{n,ab}\left(\widecheck{C}_{n},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n},\widecheck{\theta}_{n}\right)\right|\cdot\left\|\begin{pmatrix}\widecheck{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{C}_{n}-C_{n}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq O_{P}(n\log^{2}(nT))\max_{i=1,...,n}\left(\|C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}\|_{1},\|\widecheck{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}\|_{1},1\right)\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}\|_{1}+1\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 170.71652pt\times\left(\frac{1}{n}\|C_{n}^{*}-\widecheck{C}_{n}\|_{1}+\frac{1}{n}\|\alpha_{n}^{*}-\widecheck{\alpha}_{n}\|_{1}+\|\theta_{n}^{*}-\widecheck{\theta}_{n}\|_{1}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(n\log^{2}(nT)\widecheck{S}_{n}r_{n}^{2}s_{n}^{2}\right),

where
\widecheck{S}_{n}
and
r_{n}
are defined as in Section
2.1
. We hence get

\displaystyle\left\|\sqrt{nT}\Lambda_{\theta,n}\left(R_{n}-\Sigma_{n}(\widecheck{C}_{n},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n},\widecheck{\theta}_{n})\right)\begin{pmatrix}\widecheck{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{C}_{n}-C_{n}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{nT}\left\|\Lambda_{\theta,n}\right\|_{\infty}\left\|\left(R_{n}-\Sigma_{n}(\widecheck{C}_{n},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n},\widecheck{\theta}_{n})\right)\begin{pmatrix}\widecheck{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{C}_{n}-C_{n}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(n^{\frac{3}{2}}\sqrt{T}\log^{2}(nT)\widecheck{S}_{n}\left\|\Lambda_{\theta,n}\right\|_{\infty}r_{n}^{2}s_{n}^{2}\right)=o_{P}(1),

(35)

where the last step is using Assumption (B2). Furthermore, by (
9
)

\displaystyle\left\|\sqrt{nT}(J-\Lambda_{\theta,n}\Sigma_{n}(\widecheck{C}_{n},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n},\widecheck{\theta}_{n}))\begin{pmatrix}\widecheck{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{C}_{n}-C_{n}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{nT}\underset{b=1,...,p+1+n+n^{2}}{\max_{a=1,...,p+1}}\left|J_{a,b}-\left[\Lambda_{\theta,n}\Sigma_{n}(\widecheck{C}_{n},\widecheck{\alpha}_{n},\widecheck{\theta}_{n})\right]_{ab}\right|\left\|\begin{pmatrix}\widecheck{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{\alpha}_{n}-\alpha_{n}^{*}\\
\widecheck{C}_{n}-C_{n}^{*}\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(n^{\frac{3}{2}}\sqrt{T}r_{n}s_{n}\right)\max_{j=1,...,p+1}\frac{\sigma_{j}}{\tau_{j}}=o_{P}(1),

(36)

with the last equality again using Assumption (B2). Plugging (
36
) and (
35
) in (
34
) yields

\displaystyle\sqrt{nT}\left(\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{nT}\Lambda_{\theta,n}W_{n}+o_{P}(1).

(37)

We hence need to study the asymptotic behaviour of
-\sqrt{nT}\Lambda_{\theta,n}W_{n}
. Recall that
M_{n,i}
are the counting process martingales. We have by definition of
W_{n}
and
M_{n,i}

\displaystyle-\sqrt{nT}\Lambda_{\theta,n}W_{n}

\displaystyle=-\Lambda_{\theta,n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\theta}\\
\partial_{\alpha}\\
\partial_{C}\end{pmatrix}\textrm{LS}_{i}(C_{n,i}^{*},\alpha_{n,i}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*})

\displaystyle=-\Lambda_{\theta,n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Bigg(\int_{0}^{T}2\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\theta}\\
\partial_{\alpha}\\
\partial_{C}\end{pmatrix}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{n,i\cdot}^{*},\alpha_{n,i}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*})\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{n,i\cdot}^{*},\alpha_{n,i}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*})dt

\displaystyle\qquad-2\int\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\theta}\\
\partial_{\alpha}\\
\partial_{C}\end{pmatrix}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{n,i\cdot}^{*},\alpha_{n,i}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*})dN_{n,i}(t)\Bigg)

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{\theta,n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}2\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\theta}\\
\partial_{\alpha}\\
\partial_{C}\end{pmatrix}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{n,i\cdot}^{*},\alpha_{n,i}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*})dM_{n,i}(t).

(38)

We then have

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\Lambda_{\theta,n}-\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}\right)\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}2\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\theta}\\
\partial_{\alpha}\\
\partial_{C}\end{pmatrix}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{n,i\cdot}^{*},\alpha_{n,i}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*})dM_{n,i}(t)\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\Lambda_{\theta,n}-\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}\right\|_{\infty}\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}2\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\theta}\\
\partial_{\alpha}\\
\partial_{C}\end{pmatrix}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{n,i\cdot}^{*},\alpha_{n,i}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*})dM_{n,i}(t)\right\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle=O_{P}\left(\left\|\Lambda_{\theta,n}-\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}\right\|_{\infty}c_{n}\log nT\right)

\displaystyle=o_{P}(1),

by Assumption (B3) and Lemma
C.3
. Using the above in (
38
) shows that

\displaystyle-\sqrt{nT}\Lambda_{\theta,n}W_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}2\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\theta}\\
\partial_{\alpha}\\
\partial_{C}\end{pmatrix}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{n,i\cdot}^{*},\alpha_{n,i}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*})dM_{n,i}(t)+o_{P}(1).

Note that
\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}V_{n}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}^{T}
is positive definite by Assumption (B3) and hence the matrix

(\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}V_{n}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}^{T})^{-\frac{1}{2}}

is well defined. Using the previous display in (
37
), we obtain

\displaystyle\sqrt{nT}\left(\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}V_{n}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}^{T}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}\left(\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}V_{n}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}^{T}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}S_{n,i}(t)dM_{n,i}(t)+o_{P}(1)

again by Assumption (B3), where we recall that

S_{n,i}(t):=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_{\theta}\\
\partial_{\alpha}\\
\partial_{C}\end{pmatrix}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{n,i\cdot}^{*},\alpha_{n,i}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*}).

Let
a\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be arbitrary. We show now that

\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}a^{T}\left(\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}V_{n}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}^{T}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}S_{n,i}(t)dM_{n,i}(t)\overset{d}{\to}\mathcal{N}(0,\|a\|_{2}^{2}).

(39)

The main tool for the proof is Rebolledo’s Martingale Central Limit Theorem (Theorem II.5.1 in
Andersen et al. (1993)
). For an application of this theorem note first that

s\to\mathcal{M}^{n,T}(s)=\int_{0}^{sT}\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}a^{T}\left(\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}V_{n}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}^{T}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}S_{n,i}(t)dM_{n,i}(t)

is a local square integrable martingale. We apply Rebolledo’s Martingale Central Limit Theorem for one single point
\mathcal{T}_{0}=\{1\}
and verify (2.5.1) and (2.5.3) in Theorem II.5.1 in
Andersen et al. (1993)
for this choice of
\mathcal{T}_{0}
. This implies (
39
). For a check of (2.5.1) we consider
the quadratic variation process:

\displaystyle\left\langle\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}a^{T}\left(\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}V_{n}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}^{T}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}S_{n,i}(t)dM_{n,i}(t)\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}\left(a^{T}\left(\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}V_{n}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}^{T}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}S_{n,i}(t)\right)^{2}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{n,i\cdot}^{*},\alpha_{n,i}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*})dt

\displaystyle=a^{T}\left(\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}V_{n}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}^{T}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\times\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}S_{n,i}(t)S_{n,i}(t)^{T}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{n,i\cdot}^{*},\alpha_{n,i}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*})dt\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}^{T}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\left(\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}V_{n}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}^{T}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}a

\displaystyle\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\to}

\displaystyle a^{T}M_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}M_{0}M_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2}}a=\|a\|_{2}^{2},

by Assumptions (B3) and (B4). Let now
\varepsilon>0
be arbitrary. The martingale

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}a^{T}\left(\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}V_{n}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}^{T}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}S_{n,i}(t)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\times\mathbbm{1}\left(\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}a^{T}\left(\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}V_{n}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}^{T}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}S_{n,i}(t)\right|>\varepsilon\right)dM_{n,i}(t)

contains all jumps of size larger than
\varepsilon
. For a check of (2.5.3) in Theorem II.5.1 in
Andersen et al. (1993)
we have to prove that its quadratic variation converges to zero:

\displaystyle\Bigg\langle\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}a^{T}\left(\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}V_{n}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}^{T}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}S_{n,i}(t)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\mathbbm{1}\left(\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}a^{T}\left(\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}V_{n}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}^{T}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}S_{n,i}(t)\right|>\varepsilon\right)dM_{n,i}(t)\Bigg\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{nT}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}\left(a^{T}\left(\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}V_{n}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}^{T}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}S_{n,i}(t)\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\mathbbm{1}\left(\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{nT}}a^{T}\left(\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}V_{n}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}^{T}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}S_{n,i}(t)\right|>\varepsilon\right)\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{n,i\cdot}^{*},\alpha_{n,i}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*})dt

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\varepsilon(nT)^{\frac{3}{2}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\int_{0}^{T}\left|a^{T}\left(\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}V_{n}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}^{T}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\Lambda_{0,\theta,n}S_{n,i}(t)\right|^{3}\Psi_{n,i}(t;C_{n,i\cdot}^{*},\alpha_{n,i}^{*},\theta_{n}^{*})dt

\displaystyle=o_{P}(1),

by Assumption (B4). This proves (
39
) by Rebolledo’s Martingale Central Limit Theorem (see above). This, in turn, implies the statement because
a\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
was arbitrary.
∎

C.3
Proofs of Section
3.3

Proof of Lemma
3.7
.

Let
i
be fixed. By continuity, the minimum in the definition of the oracle is attained on
\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{n}(\theta)\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{H}}_{n}
. Choose an arbitrary minimizer and denote it by
C^{(i)},\alpha^{(i)}
. Note that
C^{(i)}_{j\boldsymbol{\cdot}}
and
\alpha^{(i)}_{j}
for
j\neq i
are irrelevant when it comes to computation of the objective function. This holds for all
i
. Thus, by selecting the
i
-th row of
C^{(i)}
and the
i
-th entry of
\alpha^{(i)}
, we can merge all these minimizers into a single
(C_{n}^{*}(\theta),\alpha_{n}^{*}(\theta))
at which the minimum is attained for all
i
simultaneously.
∎

Before proving Corollary
3.9
, we present a technical lemma, which we prove in Appendix
I

.

Lemma C.4
.

Let Assumptions (A1), (A2) and (A3) hold. We have that

\displaystyle\left\|\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})-\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*})\\
C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})-C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*})\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=O_{P}(1)\cdot\left(\frac{1+|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|}{\phi_{i,\textrm{comp}}(L;\theta_{n}^{*})^{2}}\log^{2}(nT)\cdot\left\|\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\right\|_{2}\left(c_{n}^{2}+\|C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\|_{1}^{2}\right)\right).

Proof of Corollary
3.9
.

We argue next that we may restrict to the event that both of the following are true:

1.

The statement of Theorem
3.8
applies for a suitable
L>0
.

2.

\inf_{i=1,...,n}\phi_{i,\textrm{comp}}(L;\overline{\theta}_{n})\geq\phi_{0}

It is enough to argue that each individual statement holds with probability converging to 1.

As for (1):
Since
\omega_{i}=3\sqrt{\log(nT)}d_{n,i}
, we have by assumption that
a_{n}/\omega_{i}=O_{P}(1)
uniformly and, hence, there is
L>0
such that
\mathbb{P}(L\geq a_{n}/\omega_{i}\textrm{ for all }i=1,...,n)\to 1
: Thus,
L>0
as required in Theorem
3.8
can be found with high probability.

Let, in addition to
a_{n}
and
d_{n,i}
, also
b_{n},e_{n}
be chosen as in Lemma
C.1
. Since

\mathcal{T}_{n}(a_{n},b_{n},d_{n},e_{n})\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{n}^{(i)}(a_{n},d_{n,i}),

we have
\mathbb{P}(\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{T}_{n}^{(i)}(a_{n},d_{n,i}))\to 1
by Lemma
C.1
.

On the intersection of these events, which has probability converging to 1, the statement of Theorem
3.8
applies.

As for (2):
By assumption,
\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\Omega_{\textrm{IRCC}}^{(i)}\left(L,\phi_{0};B_{r}(\theta_{n}^{*})\right)\right)\to 1
. Since, by Theorem
3.6
,
\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\overset{\mathbb{P}}{\to}0
, we have that
\overline{\theta}_{n}\in B_{r}(\theta_{n}^{*})
with probability converging to one for any fixed
r>0
. These together imply the statement.

For proving the rate of convergence, we may thus restrict to the event that both statements are true. For
\theta=\overline{\theta}_{n}
, we conclude, hence, from Theorem
3.8
that

\frac{1}{T}\left\|\Psi_{n,i}(\cdot;\widehat{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\widehat{\alpha}_{n,i},\overline{\theta}_{n})-\lambda_{n,i}\right\|_{T}^{2}+2\omega_{i}\|\widehat{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}-C^{*}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\|_{1}+2a_{n}|\widehat{\alpha}_{n,i}-\alpha^{*}_{n,i}(\overline{\theta}_{n})|\leq 2\varepsilon^{*}_{i}(\overline{\theta}_{n}).

(40)

Keeping in mind that
C_{n}^{*}=C_{n}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*})
, we obtain for the right hand side

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{T}\left\|\Psi_{n,i}\left(\cdot;C_{n,i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n}),\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n}),\overline{\theta}_{n}\right)-\Psi_{n,i}\left(\cdot;C_{n,i}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*}),\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*}),\theta_{n}^{*}\right)\right\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+9\frac{4\omega_{i}^{2}|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|+a_{n}^{2}}{\phi_{0}^{2}}.

(41)

We bound the first expression. Note that by Lemma
C.4
, since
\phi_{i,\textrm{comp}}(L;\theta_{n}^{*})\geq\phi_{0}>0
, and by Theorem
3.6
we have

\displaystyle\left\|\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})-\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*})\\
C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})-C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*})\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=O_{P}(1)\cdot\left(\frac{1+|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|}{\phi_{i,\textrm{comp}}(L;\theta_{n}^{*})^{2}}\log^{2}(nT)\cdot\left\|\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\right\|_{2}\left(c_{n}^{2}+\|C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\|_{1}^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=O_{P}(1)\cdot\left(\frac{1+|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|}{\sqrt{nT}}\log^{2}(nT)\left(c_{n}^{2}+\|C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\|_{1}^{2}\right)\right).

(42)

To make use of this bound, let

\displaystyle v_{i}(t;\theta):

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i}(t);\beta),&\int_{0}^{t-}g(t-r;\gamma)dN_{n,1}(r),&\dots&,\int_{0}^{t-}g(t-r;\gamma)dN_{n,n}(r)\end{pmatrix}^{T}.

so that

\Psi_{n,i}(t;c,a,\theta)=v_{i}(t;\theta)^{T}\binom{a}{c}.

Moreover, on the event
\Omega_{\mathcal{N}}
with
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{0}\log(nT)
, where
\mathcal{N}_{0}>0
is chosen such that
\mathbb{P}(\Omega_{\mathcal{N}})\to 1
by Lemma
B.2
, we can make use of the boundedness and differentiability assumptions from (A2) and (A3) to obtain the bounds

\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|v_{i}(t;\overline{\theta}_{n})-v_{i}(t;\theta_{n}^{*})\|_{\infty}\leq R_{1}\|\overline{\theta}_{n}-\theta_{n}^{*}\|\log(nT),\,\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|v_{i}(t;\theta_{n}^{*})\|_{\infty}\leq R_{2}\log(nT),

(43)

where
R_{1},R_{2}>0
are suitable constants. Finally, since
(\alpha_{n}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n}),C_{n}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n}))\in\mathcal{H}_{n}(\overline{\theta}_{n})
and
\overline{\theta}_{n}\in\Theta
, we have that
\|C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\|_{1}\leq|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|K_{C}
. This insight together with (
42
), (
43
), and Theorem
3.6
shows that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\left\|\Psi_{n,i}\left(\cdot;C_{n,i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n}),\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n}),\overline{\theta}_{n}\right)-\Psi_{n,i}\left(\cdot;C_{n,i}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*}),\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*}),\theta_{n}^{*}\right)\right\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\left\|v_{i}(t;\overline{\theta}_{n})^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\\
C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\end{pmatrix}-v_{i}(t;\theta_{n}^{*})^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*})\\
C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*})\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{T}\left\|\left(v_{i}(t;\overline{\theta}_{n})-v_{i}(t;\theta_{n}^{*})\right)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\\
C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\end{pmatrix}-v_{i}(t;\theta_{n}^{*})^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*})-\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\\
C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*})-C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{T}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{T}\left(\left\|\left(v_{i}(t;\overline{\theta}_{n})-v_{i}(t;\theta_{n}^{*})\right)^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\\
C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{T}^{2}+\left\|v_{i}(t;\theta_{n}^{*})^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*})-\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\\
C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*})-C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{T}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{T}\Bigg(T\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|v_{i}(t;\overline{\theta}_{n})-v_{i}(t;\theta_{n}^{*})\|_{\infty}^{2}\left\|\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\\
C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{1}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+T\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\|v_{i}(t;\theta_{n}^{*})\|_{\infty}^{2}\left\|\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*})-\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\\
C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*})-C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\end{pmatrix}\right\|_{1}^{2}\Bigg)

\displaystyle=O_{P}(1)\cdot\left(\frac{(1+|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|)^{2}\log^{6}(nT)}{nT}(c_{n}^{2}+\|C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}\|_{1}^{2})^{2}\right).

Using the above and the bounds on
\omega_{i}
an
a_{n}
in (
41
) yields

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})

\displaystyle=O_{P}(1)\cdot\left(\frac{(1+|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|)^{2}\log^{6}(nT)}{nT}(c_{n}^{2}+\|C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}\|_{1}^{2})^{2}\right)+O_{P}(1)\cdot\left((1+|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|)\frac{\log^{5}(nT)}{T}\right)

\displaystyle=O_{P}(1)\left((1+|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|)\frac{\log^{5}(nT)}{T}\left(\frac{(1+|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|)\log(nT)}{n}(c_{n}^{2}+\|C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}\|_{1}^{2})^{2}+1\right)\right)

\displaystyle=O_{P}(1)\cdot\left((1+|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|)\frac{\log^{5}(nT)}{T}\right),

(44)

where we used the growth condition (
12
) in the last step. Recall that
C_{n}^{*}=C_{n}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*})
and
\alpha_{n}*=\alpha_{n}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*})
. Then, using (
42
) together with the assumptions on
a_{n}
and
\omega_{i}=3\sqrt{\log(nT)}d_{n,i}
and Condition (
12
), we obtain

\displaystyle 2\omega_{i}\left\|\widehat{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}-C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}\right\|_{1}+2a_{n}\left|\widehat{\alpha}_{n,i}-\alpha_{n,i}^{*}\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\omega_{i}\left\|\widehat{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}-C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\right\|_{1}+2\omega_{i}\left\|C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})-C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*})\right\|_{1}+2a_{n}\left|\widehat{\alpha}_{n,i}-\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+2a_{n}\left|\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})-\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\theta_{n}^{*})\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\omega_{i}\left\|\widehat{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}-C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\right\|_{1}+2a_{n}\left|\widehat{\alpha}_{n,i}-\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+O_{P}(1)\cdot\left(\frac{\max(\omega_{i},a_{n})(1+|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|)\log^{2}(nT)(c_{n}^{2}+\|C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\|_{1}^{2})}{\sqrt{nT}}\right)

\displaystyle=2\omega_{i}\left\|\widehat{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}-C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\right\|_{1}+2a_{n}\left|\widehat{\alpha}_{n,i}-\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+O_{P}(1)\cdot\left(\frac{(1+|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|)\log^{\frac{9}{2}}(nT)(c_{n}^{2}+\|C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\|_{1}^{2})}{\sqrt{n}T}\right)

\displaystyle=2\omega_{i}\left\|\widehat{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}-C_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\right\|_{1}+2a_{n}\left|\widehat{\alpha}_{n,i}-\alpha_{n,i}^{*}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\right|+O_{P}(1)\cdot\left(\frac{(1+|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\log^{4}(nT)}{T}\right).

Using the above estimate together with (
40
) and (
44
) yields finally

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\left\|\Psi_{n,i}(\cdot;\widehat{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\widehat{\alpha}_{n,i},\overline{\theta}_{n})-\lambda_{n,i}\right\|_{2}^{2}+2\omega_{i}\|\widehat{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}-C^{*}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}\|_{1}+2a_{n}|\widehat{\alpha}_{n,i}-\alpha^{*}_{n,i}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{T}\left\|\Psi_{n,i}(\cdot;\widehat{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}},\widehat{\alpha}_{n,i},\overline{\theta}_{n})-\lambda_{n,i}\right\|_{2}^{2}+2\omega_{i}\|\widehat{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}-C^{*}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}(\overline{\theta}_{n})\|_{1}+2a_{n}|\widehat{\alpha}_{n,i}-\alpha^{*}_{n,i}(\overline{\theta}_{n})|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+O_{P}(1)\cdot\left(\frac{(1+|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\log^{4}(nT)}{T}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon^{*}_{i}(\overline{\theta}_{n})+O_{P}(1)\cdot\left(\frac{(1+|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\log^{4}(nT)}{T}\right)

\displaystyle=O_{P}(1)\cdot\left((1+|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|)\frac{\log^{5}(nT)}{T}\right)+O_{P}(1)\cdot\left(\frac{(1+|S_{i}(C_{n}^{*})|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\log^{4}(nT)}{T}\right),

which we wanted to prove. The stated convergence rates for
\widehat{C}_{n,i\boldsymbol{\cdot}}
and
\widehat{\alpha}_{n,i}
are then direct consequences.
∎

Appendix D
Some heuristics on the debiasing step

Here we give a heuristic discussion about why a debiasing step is needed to get an estimator of the global parameter
\theta=(\beta,\gamma)
with convergence rate
O_{P}((nT)^{-1/2})
that allows for an asymptotic distribution theory for the estimator of
\theta
. To simplify the discussion we consider the closely related model

\displaystyle Y_{n,i,t}=\alpha^{*}_{n,i}\nu_{0}(X_{n,i,t})+\sum_{j=1}^{n}C_{n,ij}^{*}g(\gamma_{n}^{*},Z_{n,ij,t})+\varepsilon_{n,i,t}\qquad(i=1,...,n;t=1,...,T),

(45)

where
(X_{n,i,t},Z_{n,ij,t}:j=1,...,n)
are some covariates,
g
is a known function and

\varepsilon_{n,i,t}
are mean zero variables that are independent of
(X_{n,i,t},Z_{n,ij,t}:j=1,...,n)
. To simplify the discussion even further we omit the first term on the right hand side of (
45
) and we arrive at the model

\displaystyle Y_{n,i,t}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}C_{n,ij}^{*}g(\gamma_{n}^{*},Z_{n,ij,t})+\varepsilon_{n,i,t}\qquad(i=1,...,n;t=1,...,T).

(46)

In this model we have a global parameter
\gamma_{n}^{*}
and local parameters
C_{n,ij}^{*}
. We now give arguments why in this model a debiasing step is needed to get an estimator of
\gamma_{n}^{*}
with rate
O_{P}((nT)^{-1/2})
.
The LASSO estimator of the parameters
C_{n,i,j}^{*}
and
\gamma_{n}^{*}
is given as the argmin
(\widehat{C}_{n,ij},\widehat{\gamma}_{n})
over
(C_{ij},\gamma)
of

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(Y_{n,i,t}-\sum_{j=1}^{n}C_{ij}g(\gamma,Z_{n,ij,t})\right)^{2}+2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{i}\|C_{i,.}\|_{1}.

For a discussion of
\widehat{C}_{n,i,j}
we now assume that
\widehat{C}_{n,i,j}
is asymptotically equivalent to the oracle estimator
\widetilde{C}_{n,i,j}
which makes use of knowing the unknown parameter
\gamma_{n}^{*}
. More precisely,
\gamma_{n}^{*}
is defined as the minimizer of

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(Y_{n,i,t}-\sum_{j=1}^{n}C_{ij}g(\gamma_{n}^{*},Z_{n,ij,t})\right)^{2}+2\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega_{i}\|C_{i.}\|_{1},

where
\gamma
is replaced by the true value
\gamma_{n}^{*}
. The oracle estimator
\widetilde{C}_{n,i,j}
